Weighted Eigenvalue Problems for Fourth-Order Operators in Degenerating Annuli
Alexis Michelat, Tristan Rivière

Abstract
We obtain a nigh optimal estimate for the first eigenvalue of two natural weighted problems associated to the bilaplacian (and of a continuous family of fourth-order elliptic operators in dimension 2 2 ) in degenerating annuli—that are central objects in bubble tree analysis—in all dimension. The estimate depends only on the conformal class of the annulus. We also show that in dimension 2 2 and dimension 4 4 , the first eigenfunction (of the first problem) is never radial provided that the conformal class of the annulus is large enough. The other result is a weighted Poincaré-type inequality in annuli for those fourth-order operators. Applications to Morse theory are given.



Mathematical subject classification :
15A18, 15A42, 34L15, 35P15, 58J05, 58E05, 35A15, 35J20.

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivation and Main Results

In
[
8
]
, a new approach is developed to show the upper semi-continuity of the extended Morse index—the number of negative or null eigenvalues—of sequences of critical point of geometric functionals with uniformly bounded energy. One of the main steps in the argument is to derive suitable weighted estimates in degenerating annuli. Namely, if
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, then for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, Da Lio-Gianocca-Rivière showed (
[
8
, Lemma IV.
1
]
) that for all
0<\beta<1
the follows estimate holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\geq C_{\beta}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)dx,

where
0<C_{\beta}<\infty
is a universal constant. More precisely, the more precise inequality holds for all
u\in W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
(see
[
8
]
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\geq\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

This estimate is central to prove that the neck regions—associated to bubble trees of fourth-order equations such as Willmore surfaces (in dimension
2
) and the (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic maps in dimension
4
—contribute positively to the second derivative. For more details, refer to the introduction of
[
13
]
. It is equivalent to the estimate of the first positive eigenvalue for the problem

\displaystyle-\Delta u=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{|x|^{2}}u\qquad\text{in}\;\,\Omega,

that is related to the Hardy’s inequality in dimension
d\geq 3
(
[
7
]
). Due to the singularity of the weight
\dfrac{1}{|x|^{2}}
, this inequality does not hold in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but the analysis of
[
8
]
furnishes the precise asymptotic behaviour of the degeneracy of this constant on annuli whose conformal classes diverges to
\infty
.

In this article, we generalise this estimate for a natural family of fourth-order operators in dimension
2
, and in all dimension in the case of the bilaplacian
\Delta^{2}
. Namely, we prove that for all
m\geq 1
, and for all
1/2<\beta<1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx\geq C_{\beta,m}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{4\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{4\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)dx

and for all
\sqrt{2}-1<\beta<1

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}\geq C_{\beta,m}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{|x|}{b}\right)^{2\beta}+\left(\frac{a}{|x|}\right)^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)dx.

Let us point out that our inequality is more general provided that
m>1
.
The operator

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}=|x|^{1-m}\Delta\left(|x|^{m-1}\,\cdot\,\right)

naturally appears when on studies the Morse index stability of branched surfaces (and its adjoint plays an important role in the study of the index of branched Willmore spheres
[
10
]
). At a branch point in a neck region, thanks to the analysis of
[
15
]
the metric admits the following expansion in a conformal chart

\displaystyle g=e^{2u}|z|^{2m-2}|dz|^{2},

where
u
is a bounded function and
m\geq 1
is a positive integer. Since the Willmore energy is a non-linear biharmonic equation, one can easily show that the dominant term of the second derivative (we do not give a precise definition of this quadratic functional and refer to the introduction of
[
13
]
for more details) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(\Delta_{g}u+|A|^{2}u)^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
A
is the second fundamental form. Using the previous expansion, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta_{g}u)^{2}d\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\Omega}e^{-2u}(|x|^{1-m}\Delta u)^{2}dx.

Since we want to show that this positive term controls the lower-order terms of the second derivative, making the change of variable
u=|x|^{m-1}v
, we are led to the previous estimates. In the case of biharmonic maps, we show a general eigenvalue estimate in all dimension.

We can now state the main theorems more precisely.

Theorem A
.

Let
m\geq 1
, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consider the following minimisation problem

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}=\inf\left\{\int_{\Omega}(\mathscr{L}_{m}u)^{2}dx:u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\;\,\text{and}\;\,\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=1\right\}.

(1.1)

Then, there exists a unique solution
u
to (
1.1
), that satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}u+\frac{2(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{2}}\Delta u-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}u\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}u=\frac{\lambda_{m}}{|x|^{4}}u,

and there exists a constant
R_{1,m}<\infty
such that provided that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{1,m},

(1.2)

then

\displaystyle\left(4m^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}<\lambda_{m}<\left(4m^{2}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}.

(1.3)

Furthermore, if
m\geq 1
is an integer, there exists
R_{2,m}<\infty
such that the condition

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{2,m}

implies that the solution
u
takes the form
u(r,\theta)=\mathrm{Re}\,\left(u_{0}(r)e^{im\theta}\right)
, where
u_{0}
is a solution of the following ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{2}{r}f^{\prime\prime\prime}-(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}f^{\prime\prime}+(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}f^{\prime}-(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{4}}f=\frac{\lambda_{m}}{r^{4}}f.

(1.4)

Remark 1.1
.

(
1
)

In contrast to the previously quoted result from
[
8
]
, the bound on the first eigenvalue of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}
for the weighted problem is only true asymptotically, and the optimal solution is
not
radial, even for
m=1
where
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}
is the bi-Laplacian! This result is reminiscent to those on Ginzburg-Landau vortices where the solution is radial up to an
e^{i\,\theta}
phase (
[
1
,
3
]
).

(
2
)

The bound on the conformal class is far from optimal, but we are mostly interested in the estimate when the conformal class diverges.

(
3
)

More precisely, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left(4m^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}<\lambda_{m}<\left(4m^{2}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}.

The proof is based on a delicate ODE analysis of all frequencies in Fourier space (the same approach works in higher dimension if we replace the
e^{ik\theta}
functions (
k\in\mathbb{Z}
) by spherical harmonics), that notably proves that the optimal solution is
not
radial, but has a phase
e^{im\theta}
provided that
m\geq 1
is integer and the conformal class is large enough. It amounts at studying a family of ODEs and estimating the first positive zero of an explicit (and non-trivial!) function of the conformal class. Even the case
m=1
of the bilaplacian was not known, and furnishes an interesting counter-example to the classical Gidas-Ni-Nirenberg theorem (
[
6
]
) that shows under general hypotheses that the first eigenfunction must be radial (the theorem does not apply here since our domain is not simply connected).

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
m\geq 1
, let
0<a<b<\infty
and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. Assume that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2m-1}}

(1.5)

Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx\geq\left(4m^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx.

(1.6)

Remark 1.3
.

1
.

In the case of the bilaplacian
\mathscr{L}_{1}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{1}=\Delta^{2}
, the condition on
a<b
becomes

\displaystyle\frac{b}{a}\geq e^{\pi\sqrt{2}}=85.019695\cdots

2
.

This kind of Rellich inequality for general elliptic operators with regular singularities have already been considered in higher dimension
d\geq 5
(
[
9
]
) and in dimension
d=3
for the subclass of functions
W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap\left\{u:u(0)=0\right\}
that vanish at the origin.

A similar result holds for the second minimisation problem.

Theorem B
.

Let
m\geq 1
, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consider the following minimisation problem

\displaystyle\mu_{m}=\inf\left\{\int_{\Omega}(\mathscr{L}_{m}u)^{2}dx:u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\;\,\text{and}\;\,\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx=1\right\}.

(1.7)

Then, there exists a unique solution
u
to (
1.7
) that satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}u+\frac{2(m^{2}-1)}{|x|^{2}}\Delta u-4(m^{2}-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}u\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m^{2}-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}u=-\frac{\mu_{m}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\Delta u-2\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u\right),

and there exists a constant
R_{1,m}^{\ast}<\infty
such that provided that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{1,m}^{\ast},

(1.8)

then

\displaystyle\dfrac{\left(4m^{2}+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(m^{2}+1)+\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}<\mu_{m}<\dfrac{\left(4m^{2}+\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{m^{2}+1+\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}.

(1.9)

Furthermore, if
m\geq 1
is an integer, there exists
R_{2,m}^{\ast}<\infty
such that the condition

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{2,m}^{\ast}

the solution
u
takes the form
u(r,\theta)=\mathrm{Re}\,\left(u_{0}(r)e^{im\theta}\right)
, where
u_{0}
is a solution of the following ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{2}{r}f^{\prime\prime\prime}-(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}f^{\prime\prime}+(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}f^{\prime}-(4m^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{4}}f=-\frac{\mu_{m}}{r^{4}}\left(f^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{r}f^{\prime}-\frac{m^{2}}{r^{2}}f\right).

(1.10)

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
m\geq 1
, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Assume that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{1,m}^{\ast}.

(1.11)

Then, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}u\right)^{2}dx\geq\dfrac{\left(4m^{2}+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(m^{2}+1)+\dfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(1.12)

Finally, the general result for the bilaplacian in all dimension reads as follows. For simplicity, we only state the inequality that comes out of corollary from the eigenvalue estimate.

Theorem C
.

Let
d\geq 3
, let
0<a<b<\infty
, and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, there exists
R_{d}<\infty
such that the hypothesis

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq R_{d}

implies that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\left(\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left(\frac{(d-4)^{2}}{4}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx.

(1.13)

Furthermore, for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
and
d\geq 5
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\frac{d^{2}(d-4)^{2}+((d-2)^{2}+4)\dfrac{8\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{4}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(d-4)^{2}+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

(1.14)

For
d=3
, and for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{2,2}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\frac{25+\dfrac{108\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{36+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

Finally, for
d=4
, and for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\frac{9+\dfrac{10\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{3+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx.

The first inequality was already known in the full space
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the case
d\geq 5
and bears the name of Rellich inequality (
[
17
]
). Furthermore, our analysis allows us to recover (thanks to an explicit upper bound of the first eigenvalue) the optimal constant
in the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\left(\frac{d(d-4)}{4}\right)^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx

valid for
d\geq 5
and
u\in W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(refer to
[
20
]
,
[
4
]
, and the survey paper
[
5
]
for some history of those inequalities. For
d=3
, we deduce that for all
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\left\{0\right\})

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq\frac{9}{16}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx.

(1.15)

By the Sobolev embedding
W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow W^{1,6}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\hookrightarrow C^{0,\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\overline{C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\left\{0\right\})}^{W^{2,2}}=W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\cap\left\{u:u(0)=0\right\},

and we deduce that for all
u\in W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
u(0)=0
, the optimal inequality (
1.15
) holds (which recovers a result of Rellich
[
17
]
; see also
[
9
]
). As expected, for
d=2
or
d=4
, we do not get any new inequality (in the first case, the integral would not be finite in general, and in the second case, by density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\left\{0\right\})
in
W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, it would imply an inequality for all functions
u\in W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, which is impossible since
|x|^{-4}\notin{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
).

The second inequality bears the name Hardy-Rellich inequality, and we deduce that for all
d\geq 3
and for all
u\in W^{2,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\geq c_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\nabla u|^{2}}{|x|^{2}}dx

(1.16)

where

\displaystyle c_{d}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\frac{25}{36}\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d=3\\
&3\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d=4\\
&\frac{d^{2}}{4}\qquad&&\text{for}\;\,d\geq 5,\end{aligned}\right.

which is the optimal constant in the Hardy-Rellich inequality (
[
5
]
). Furthermore, we recover the fact that if
d=3,4
, the minimiser of the inequality (in the annulus) is of the form
u(r,\theta)=u_{0}(r)Y_{1}(\theta)
, where
Y_{1}
is the first spherical harmonic on
S^{d}
, while the minimiser is radial for
d\geq 5
. We indeed show that for
d\neq 4
, the first eigenvalue can be bounded from below by

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}>\inf_{n\geq 0}\left\{\frac{\left(d(d-4)+4n(n+d-2)\right)^{2}+((d-2)^{2}+4+4n(n+d-2))\dfrac{8\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{4(d-4)^{2}+16n(n+d-2)+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\right\}

which is minimal for
n=0
if
d\geq 5
, and for
n=1
if
d=3
(provided that the conformal class is large enough). For
d=4
, the formula above is valid for
n\geq 1
, but due to an exceptional algebraic structure, we prove that for
n=0
, the first eigenvalue of the associated ODE is equal (this is the only case where one gets an exact expression of the first eigenvalue) to

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=4+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}

which is strictly larger than

\displaystyle\inf_{n\geq 1}\left\{\frac{16n^{2}(n+2)^{2}+(8+4n(n+2))\dfrac{8\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{16\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{16n(n+2)+\dfrac{16\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}\right\}=\frac{9+\dfrac{10\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}+\dfrac{\pi^{4}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}{3+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}}=3+O\left(\frac{1}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)

if the conformal class is large enough.

1.2
Applications

As we mentioned it in the beginning of the introduction, the main motivation behind this work is applications to Morse theory. More precisely, the estimate proven for the bilaplacian
\Delta^{2}
in dimension
2
allow one to prove the upper semi-continuity of the extended Morse index in the case of immersions (
[
13
]
), whiles the estimate on
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}
(for
m\geq 2
) should allow us to treat the case of branched immersions (
[
14
]
)—there are many technical difficulties in this case, starting by the definition of the Morse index—and later on to adapt our methods to the viscosity method (
[
11
]
). Furthermore, the results in dimension
4
for the bilaplacian should easily give us the two-sided Morse index stability estimate for (intrinsic or extrinsic) biharmonic maps into manifolds (
[
12
]
). Finally, it should be possible to use the eigenvalues estimates to prove Morse index stability results for the Yang-Mills functional. Indeed, in an appropriate gauge (
[
21
,
18
]
), the highest order term in the Yang-Mills functional becomes the
L^{2}
norm of the Laplacian of a suitable function, which should allow one to prove that variations located in the neck have a positive contributions in the second derivative (that remains to be computed).

2
First Eigenvalue Problem in Dimension 2

2.1
Basic Properties of the Family of Differential Operators

As we mentioned it in the introduction, we will study some fine properties of the following second-order elliptic differential operator with
regular
singularities (
[
16
]
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}.

(2.1)

As we mentioned in the introduction, its adjoint we already studied in
[
10
]
. Let us recall some basic properties of
\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}
.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
10
]
)
.

0<a<b<\infty
, and let
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. Define for all for all
m\geq 1
the second order elliptic differential operator

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}=\Delta-2(m+1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m+1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}.

Let
u\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)
be such that
u=\partial_{\nu}u=0
Then we have the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u-2(m+1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+(m+1)^{2}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx

(2.2)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+(m+1)(m-1)\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx+4(m+1)(m-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx.

Remark 2.2
.

With the previous formula, we have
\mathscr{L}_{m}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m-2}^{\ast}
, since

\displaystyle\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla=\nabla\log|x|\cdot\nabla,

and
\log|\,\cdot\,|
is a harmonic function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
.

We will also need the following computation from
[
10
]
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}^{\ast}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+\frac{2(m+1)(m-1)}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m+1)(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}},

(2.3)

and we indeed recover
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{1}^{\ast}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{1}=\Delta^{2}
in the case
m=1
.

2.2
Computation of the Fourth-Order Differential Operators

The goal of this section is to prove the following result:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}^{\ast}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}.

By the formulae (from
[
10
]
)

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}(\,\cdot\,)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\left(\Delta-4\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{4}{|x|^{2}}\right)u

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla(\,\cdot\,)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta+\frac{2}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right),

we get

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathscr{L}_{m})

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta+4(m-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-8(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}\left(\Delta-4\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{4}{|x|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta+(m-1)(m+3)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-8(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-4(m-1)^{2}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{4(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

Likewise, by the identities

\displaystyle\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\right)=\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)-\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla

\displaystyle\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}(\,\cdot\,)\right)=\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{2}{|x|^{4}},

we get

\displaystyle\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla(\mathscr{L}_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta+2(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)-2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla

\displaystyle+(m-1)^{2}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla-\frac{2(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}

Trivially, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

Using the identity
\dfrac{x}{|x|^{2}}=\nabla\log|x|
, we obyain

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}=\Delta-2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{2}}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\Delta^{2}+\cancel{2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta}+(m-1)(m+3)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-8(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-\cancel{4(m-1)^{2}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla}+\frac{4(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}-\cancel{2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla\Delta}-4(m-1)^{2}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\cancel{4(m-1)^{2}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla}-\cancel{2(m-1)^{3}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla}+\frac{4(m-1)^{3}}{|x|^{2}}+\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}\Delta+\cancel{2(m-1)^{3}\frac{x}{|x|^{4}}\cdot\nabla}+\frac{(m-1)^{4}}{|x|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{2}+2(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\Delta-4(m+1)(m-1)\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)+\frac{(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}}{|x|^{4}}.

(2.4)

Comparing (
2.3
) and (
2.2
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}^{\ast}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m}=\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m-2}\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}_{m-2}^{\ast}.

(2.5)

2.3
Estimates on the Second-Order Differential Operators

Thanks to (
2.2
), if
0<a<b<\infty
,
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
, and
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, then we get by (
2.2
)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\mathscr{L}_{m}u)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+(m^{2}-1)\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx.

(2.6)

2.4
Expression of the Second-Order Differential Operators in Polar Coordinates

Using polar coordinates

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x_{1}}f\\
\partial_{x_{2}}f\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta)\partial_{r}f-\sin(\theta)\dfrac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}f\vskip 5.0pt\\
\sin(\theta)\partial_{r}f+\cos(\theta)\dfrac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}f\end{pmatrix},

we get

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}f

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}(\theta)\partial_{r}^{2}f+\sin^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f-2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f+\sin^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f+2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{1},x_{2}}^{2}f

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\left(\partial_{r}^{2}f-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f\right)+\left(\cos^{2}(\theta)-\sin^{2}(\theta)\right)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f-\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f

\displaystyle+\left(\sin^{2}(\theta)-\cos^{2}(\theta)\right)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}f

\displaystyle=\sin^{2}(\theta)\partial_{r}^{2}f+\cos^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f+2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f+\cos^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f-2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle|x|^{2}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}f\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}x_{i}\,x_{j}\,\partial_{x_{i},x_{j}}^{2}u

\displaystyle=\cos^{2}(\theta)\left(\cos^{2}(\theta)\partial_{r}^{2}f+\cancel{\sin^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f}-\cancel{2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f}+\cancel{\sin^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f}+\cancel{2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f}\right)

\displaystyle+2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\left(\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\left(\partial_{r}^{2}f-\cancel{\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f}\right)+\cancel{\left(\cos^{2}(\theta)-\sin^{2}(\theta)\right)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f}-\cancel{\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\cancel{\left(\sin^{2}(\theta)-\cos^{2}(\theta)\right)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f}\right)+\sin^{2}(\theta)\left(\sin^{2}(\theta)\partial_{r}^{2}f+\cancel{\cos^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}f}+\cancel{2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r,\theta}^{2}f}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\cancel{\cos^{2}(\theta)\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f}-\cancel{2\cos(\theta)\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}f}\right)=\partial_{r}^{2}f,

so that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{t}\cdot\nabla^{2}f\cdot\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}f

(2.7)

where we used the identity

\cos^{4}(\theta)+2\cos^{2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\theta)+\sin^{4}(\theta)=(\cos^{2}(\theta)+\sin^{2}(\theta))^{2}=1.
By the identity

\Delta=\partial_{r}^{2}+\dfrac{1}{r}\partial_{r}+\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2},
we get

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\Delta=\partial_{r}^{3}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\Delta=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{4}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{6}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}\Delta=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}.

Therefore, we finally deduce that

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{4}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{6}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{4}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{4}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}\partial_{\theta}^{2}+\frac{4}{r^{4}}\partial_{\theta}^{2}

Projecting on the eigenspace corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda=n^{2}
, we get the operator

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}\left(\Delta^{2}\right)=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2\,n^{2}+1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2\,n^{2}+1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{n^{4}-4n^{2}}{r^{4}}.

Since

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\Delta)=\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}-\frac{n^{2}}{r^{2}},

we finally get by (
2.7
)

\displaystyle\Pi_{n^{2}}(\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m})=\Pi_{n^{2}}\left(\Delta^{2}+2(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta-4(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}}{r^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-\frac{2\,n^{2}+1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{2\,n^{2}+1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}+\frac{n^{4}-4n^{2}}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle+2(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+2(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}-2n^{2}(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{4}}-4(m+1)(m-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}}{r^{4}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}^{4}+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}-(2(m+1)(m-1)+2\,n^{2}+1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}+(2(m+1)(m-1)+2\,n^{2}+1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}

\displaystyle+\left((m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m+1)(m-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)\right)\frac{1}{r^{4}}.

(2.8)

2.5
Minimisation Problem and Main Theorem

2.5.1
Existence and Non-Triviality of Minimisers

Let
m\geq 1
be a fixed real number, and consider for all
0<a<b<\infty
the following minimisation problem:

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}=\inf\left\{\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx:u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0))\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=1\right\}.

(2.9)

From now on, we write
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
.
For
m=1
, as
\mathscr{L}_{1}=\Delta
, there exists a constant
C=C(\Omega)
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}\right)dx\leq C\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx.

Indeed, by the standard Poincaré inequality, there exists a universal constant
C(\Omega)<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{2}dx\leq C(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

(2.10)

Integrating by parts, and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
2.10
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx=-\int_{\Omega}u\,\Delta u\,dx\leq\left(\int_{\Omega}u^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left(C(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore, provided that
u
is not constant (
i.e.

u\neq 0
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\leq C(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx,

which implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}\right)dx\leq C(\Omega)(1+C(\Omega))\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx.

(2.11)

Finally, by integrating by parts, we have for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}|dz|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}^{2}u\,\partial_{\overline{z}}^{2}u\,|dz|^{2}=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{\overline{z}}(\partial_{z}^{2}u)\partial_{\overline{z}}u\,|dz|^{2}=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{z}(\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}u)\partial_{\overline{z}}u\,|dz|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}u|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx,

using the well-known formula
\Delta=4\,\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(2|\partial_{z}^{2}u|^{2}+2|\partial_{z\overline{z}}^{2}u|^{2}\right)|dz|^{2}=\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx.

(2.12)

Gathering (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we deduce that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}\right)dx\leq\left(1+C(\Omega)\right)^{2}\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx.

(2.13)

where
C(\Omega)<\infty
is the Poincaré constant from (
2.10
).
Therefore,
E(u)=\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
is a coercive quadratic form, which shows by the Lax-Milgram theorem (
[
2
, Corollaire V.
8
]
) that there exists a unique minimiser to this problem. Let us denote it by
u
. It satisfies for some
\lambda_{1}\geq 0
the equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta^{2}u&=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{|x|^{4}}u\quad&&\text{in}\;\,\Omega\\
u&=0\quad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial\Omega\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=0\quad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.14)

Integrating by parts, we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}u\,\Delta^{2}u\,dx=\lambda_{1}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=\lambda_{1},

(2.15)

since

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=1

by construction (more precisely, thanks to the compact Sobolev embedding
W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)
; see the computations below for more details in general).

Now let us generalise (
2.13
) to the general case
m\geq 1
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
0<a<b<\infty
,
m\geq 1
, and
\Omega=B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)
. Then, there exists a universal constant
C=C(m,\Omega)
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(|\nabla^{2}u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2}\right)dx\leq C\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+2(m-1)\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u+(m-1)^{2}\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx.

(2.16)

Proof.

We have already treated the case
m=1
, so we can assume that
m>1
. Thanks to (
2.6
), we have for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx=\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+(m^{2}-1)\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx+4(m^{2}-1)\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{|x|^{2}}\cdot\nabla u-\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx.

(2.17)

Thanks to the Poincaré inequality, there exists
C(\Omega)<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)\subset W^{1,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{2}dx\leq C(\Omega)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

Therefore, the following elementary inequality holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx\leq\frac{1}{a^{4}}\int_{\Omega}u^{2}dx\leq\frac{C(\Omega)}{a^{4}}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}dx.

(2.18)

Therefore, we deduce by (
2.18
), (
2.17
), and Minkowski’s inequality that

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq(m^{2}-1)\left\|\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\Delta u+(m^{2}-1)\frac{u}{|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq(m^{2}-1)\frac{\sqrt{C(\Omega)}}{a^{2}}\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)}.

Finally, using (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we deduce that there exists
C(\Omega)<\infty
such that for all
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{2,2}(\Omega)}\leq\sqrt{C(\Omega)}\left\|\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\Omega)},

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

As previously, we deduce that there exists a minimiser
u
to (
2.9
). Indeed, take a minimising sequence
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
, where

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u_{k}^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=1

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u_{k}\right)^{2}dx\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}\lambda_{m}.

Then, by inequality (
2.16
),
\left\{u_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence in
W^{2,2}(\Omega)
. In particular, we deduce that there exists
u\in W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle u_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\rightharpoonup}u\;\,\text{weakly in}\;\,W^{2,2}_{0}(\Omega).

Thanks to the compact Sobolev embedding of
W^{1,2}(\Omega)\lhook\joinrel\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L^{q}(\Omega)
for all
q<\infty
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|u_{k}-u\right\|_{\mathrm{W}^{1,2}(\Omega)}=0.

In particular, the constraint

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\frac{u_{k}^{2}}{|x|^{4}}=1

(2.19)

is satisfied, and Fatou’s lemma shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx=\lambda_{m}.

(2.20)

By the constraint (
2.19
) and the bound (
2.20
), we deduce that
u
is a minimiser and that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\mathscr{L}_{m}u\right)^{2}dx=\lambda_{m}.

The solution
u
is a solution to the following system of equations

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}u&=\frac{\lambda}{|x|^{4}}u\quad&&\text{in}\;\,\Omega\\
u&=0\quad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial\Omega\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=0\quad&&\text{on}\;\,\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.21)

Integrating by parts, we recover

\displaystyle\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}(\mathscr{L}_{m}u)^{2}dx=\lambda_{m}\int_{B_{b}\setminus\overline{B}_{a}(0)}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=\lambda_{m}.

(2.22)

Lemma 2.4
.

Under the previous hypothesis, for all
m\geq 1
, we have
\lambda_{m}>0
.

Proof.

Indeed, if
\lambda_{m}=0
, then
\mathscr{L}_{m}=0
, and inequality (
2.16
) shows that
u=0
, which contradicts the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{u^{2}}{|x|^{4}}dx=1.

∎

2.5.2
Strategy of the Proof

The proof goes as follows.
We study the solutions of the equation

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{m}^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{m}f=\frac{\lambda}{|x|^{4}}f

(2.23)

projected on the eigenspace of the eigenvector
e^{i\,n\,\theta}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. That leads us to study thanks to (
2.4
) the solutions of the following ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{2}{r}f^{\prime\prime\prime}-\left(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1\right)\frac{1}{r^{2}}f^{\prime\prime}+\left(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1\right)\frac{1}{r^{3}}f^{\prime}+\left((m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2})\right)\frac{1}{r^{4}}f=\frac{\lambda}{r^{4}}f.

(2.24)

with the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &f(a)=f(b)=0\\
&f^{\prime}(a)=f^{\prime}(b)=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(2.25)

that are equivalent to
u=\partial_{\nu}u=0
on
\partial\Omega
for
u=f(r)e^{i\,n\,\theta}
. In order to get the previous equation, we used the elementary identities:

\displaystyle 2(m+1)(m-1)+2n^{2}+1

\displaystyle=2(m^{2}-1)+2n^{2}+1=2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1

\displaystyle(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m+1)(m-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)

\displaystyle=(m^{2}-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m^{2}-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)

\displaystyle=(m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}

Then, a careful analysis gives us a bound on the minimal coefficient
\lambda=\lambda_{m,n}
of this equation, once we make a change of variable that makes it linear (this is where the regular singularities of our operator play a crucial role).

2.6
Linearisation of the Ordinary Differential Equation

Let
m>0
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and consider non-trivial solutions of the following ordinary differential equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{4}f+\frac{2}{r}\partial_{r}^{3}f-(2(m+1)(m-1)+2\,n^{2}+1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{r}^{2}f+(2(m+1)(m-1)+2\,n^{2}+1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}\partial_{r}f

\displaystyle+\left((m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m+1)(m-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)\right)\frac{1}{r^{4}}f=\frac{\lambda}{r^{4}}f,

(2.26)

where
\lambda\geq 0
.
Notice that

\displaystyle(m+1)^{2}(m-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m+1)(m-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)

\displaystyle=(m^{2}-1)^{2}-2n^{2}(m^{2}-1)+n^{2}(n^{2}-4)=(m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}.

Make the change of variable
f(r)=Y(\log(r))
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{r}f(r)=\frac{1}{r}Y^{\prime}(\log(r))\\
&\partial_{r}^{2}f(r)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))-\frac{1}{r^{2}}Y^{\prime}(\log(r))\\
&\partial_{r}^{3}f(r)=\frac{1}{r^{3}}Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-\frac{3}{r^{3}}Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))+\frac{2}{r^{3}}Y^{\prime}(\log(r))\\
&\partial_{r}^{4}f(r)=\frac{1}{r^{4}}Y^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-\frac{6}{r^{4}}Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))+\frac{11}{r^{4}}Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))-\frac{6}{r^{4}}Y^{\prime}(\log(r)).\end{aligned}\right.

(2.27)

Therefore, (
2.6
) and (
2.27
) show that

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+\frac{2}{r}f^{\prime\prime\prime}-(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{2}}f^{\prime\prime}+(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1)\frac{1}{r^{3}}f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\bigg(Y^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-6\,Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))+11\,Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))-6\,Y^{\prime}(\log(r))

\displaystyle+2\,Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-6\,Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))+4\,Y^{\prime}(\log(r))-(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))+(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime}(\log(r))

\displaystyle+(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime}(\log(r))\bigg)=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\bigg(Y^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-4\,Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(\log(r))-(2m^{2}+2n^{2}-6)Y^{\prime\prime}(\log(r))

\displaystyle+4(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime}(\log(r))\bigg)=\frac{\lambda-\left((m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}\right)}{r^{4}}Y(\log(r)).

(2.28)

If
r=e^{t}
, then the equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle Y^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(t)-4\,Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)-2(m^{2}+n^{2}-3)Y^{\prime\prime}(t)+4(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda-\left((m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}\right)\right)Y(t).

(2.29)

Its characteristic polynomial is given by

\displaystyle P(X)=X^{4}-4X^{3}-2(m^{2}+n^{2}-3)X^{2}+4(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)X+(m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}-\lambda,

and the roots of this polynomial are given by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} r_{1}&=1+\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}+\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}\\
r_{2}&=1-\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}-\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}\\
r_{3}&=1+\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}+\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}\\
r_{4}&=1-\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}-\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.30)

Indeed, if we make the substitution
X=Y+1
, we obtain

\displaystyle Q(Y)=P(Y+1)=Y^{4}+4Y^{3}+6Y^{2}+4Y+1-4\left(Y^{3}+3Y^{2}+3Y+1\right)

\displaystyle-2(m^{2}+n^{2}-3)\left(Y^{2}+2Y+1\right)+4(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)(Y+1)+(m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}-\lambda

\displaystyle=Y^{4}-2(m^{2}+n^{2})Y^{2}+1+2(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)+(m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}-\lambda

\displaystyle=Y^{4}-2(m^{2}+n^{2})Y^{2}+(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}-\lambda

(2.31)

which is a biquadratic equation!
Since
(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}=(m^{2}+n^{2})^{2}-4m^{2}n^{2}
, we deduce that the discriminant of the polynomial
R(Z)=Q(\sqrt{Z})
is given by

\displaystyle D_{m,n}=4(m^{2}+n^{2})^{2}-4\left((m^{2}+n^{2})^{2}-\lambda-4m^{2}n^{2}\right)=4(\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}),

which shows that the roots of
R
are given by

\displaystyle s_{\pm}=m^{2}+n^{2}\pm\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}},

and we obtain the formula (
2.30
) for the roots of
P
(using that
Z=Y^{2}
and
X=Y+1
).

2.7
Proof of the Main Results

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
m>0
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
0<a<b<\infty
be such that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)>\frac{5}{\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}.

(2.32)

For all
\lambda>0
, consider the following linear differential equation

\displaystyle Y^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(t)-4\,Y^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)-2(m^{2}+n^{2}-3)Y^{\prime\prime}(t)+4(m^{2}+n^{2}-1)Y^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda-\left((m^{2}-n^{2}-1)^{2}-4n^{2}\right)\right)Y(t)=0.

(2.33)

with boundary conditions

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} Y(\log(a))&=Y(\log(b))&&=0\\
Y^{\prime}(\log(a))&=Y^{\prime}(\log(b))&&=0\end{aligned}\right.

(2.34)

There exists
\lambda>0
such that the system (
2.5
)-(
2.34
) admits a non-trivial solution
Y
. Furthermore, the minimal value
\lambda_{m,n}>0
satisfies the following estimate

\displaystyle\left((m-n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left((m+n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)<\lambda_{m,n}<\left((m-n)^{2}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left((m+n)^{2}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right).

(2.35)

Assuming that
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2m-1}},

(2.36)

we have

\displaystyle\inf_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda_{m,n}>\left(4m^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}.

(2.37)

Proof.

Notice that

\displaystyle m^{2}+n^{2}-\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}\leq 0

if and only if

\displaystyle m^{4}+2m^{2}n^{2}+n^{4}\leq\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2},

or

\displaystyle\lambda\geq m^{4}-2m^{2}n^{2}+n^{4}=(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}.

Therefore, we need to distinguish three cases.

Case 1:

\lambda<(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}
.

Then,
P
has four distinct real roots, and the solution
Y
is given by

\displaystyle Y(t)=\mu_{1}e^{r_{1}t}+\mu_{2}e^{r_{2}t}+\mu_{3}e^{r_{3}t}+\mu_{4}e^{r_{4}t}.

Since

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}(t)=r_{1}\,\mu_{1}e^{r_{1}t}+r_{2}\,\mu_{2}e^{r_{2}t}+r_{3}\,\mu_{3}e^{r_{3}t}+r_{4}\,\mu_{4}e^{r_{2}t},

the boundary conditions (
2.34
) show that the following linear system holds true:

\displaystyle A\,\mu=\begin{pmatrix}a^{r_{1}}&a^{r_{2}}&a^{r_{3}}&a^{r_{4}}\\
b^{r_{1}}&b^{r_{2}}&b^{r_{3}}&b^{r_{4}}\\
r_{1}\,a^{r_{1}}&r_{2}\,a^{r_{2}}&r_{3}\,a^{r_{3}}&r_{4}\,a^{r_{4}}\\
r_{1}\,b^{r_{2}}&r_{2}\,b^{r_{2}}&r_{3}\,b^{r_{3}}&r_{4}\,b^{r_{4}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\\
\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{4}\end{pmatrix}=0.

Now write for simplicity

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &r_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}\\
&r_{2}=1-\lambda_{1}\\
&r_{3}=1+\lambda_{2}\\
&r_{4}=1-\lambda_{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\lambda_{1}=\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}+\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}
, and
\lambda_{2}=\sqrt{m^{2}+n^{2}-\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}}
.

In order to make computations more readable, define for
X,Y\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}&X^{\delta}\\
Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}&Y^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,X^{\alpha}&\beta\,X^{\beta}&\gamma\,X^{\gamma}&\delta\,X^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,Y^{\alpha}&\beta\,Y^{\beta}&\gamma\,Y^{\gamma}&\delta\,Y^{\delta}\end{pmatrix}.

We have

\displaystyle\det(B)

\displaystyle=X^{\alpha}\begin{vmatrix}Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}&Y^{\delta}\\
\beta\,X^{\beta}&\gamma\,X^{\gamma}&\delta\,X^{\delta}\\
\beta\,Y^{\beta}&\gamma\,Y^{\gamma}&\delta\,Y^{\delta}\end{vmatrix}-X^{\beta}\begin{vmatrix}Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\gamma}&Y^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,X^{\alpha}&\gamma\,X^{\gamma}&\delta\,X^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,Y^{\alpha}&\gamma\,Y^{\gamma}&\delta\,Y^{\delta}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle+X^{\gamma}\begin{vmatrix}Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,X^{\alpha}&\beta\,X^{\beta}&\delta\,X^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,Y^{\alpha}&\beta\,Y^{\beta}&\delta\,Y^{\delta}\end{vmatrix}-X^{\delta}\begin{vmatrix}Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}\\
\alpha\,X^{\alpha}&\beta\,X^{\beta}&\gamma\,X^{\gamma}\\
\alpha\,Y^{\alpha}&\beta\,Y^{\beta}&\gamma\,Y^{\gamma}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=X^{\alpha}\Big(\beta(\gamma-\delta)X^{\beta}Y^{\gamma+\delta}+\gamma(\delta-\beta)X^{\gamma}Y^{\beta+\delta}+\delta(\beta-\gamma)X^{\delta}Y^{\beta+\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle-X^{\beta}\Big(\alpha(\gamma-\delta)X^{\alpha}Y^{\gamma+\delta}+\gamma(\delta-\alpha)X^{\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\delta}+\delta(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\delta}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle+X^{\gamma}\Big(\alpha(\beta-\delta)X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta+\delta}+\beta(\delta-\alpha)X^{\beta}Y^{\alpha+\delta}+\delta(\alpha-\beta)X^{\delta}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle-X^{\delta}\Big(\alpha(\beta-\gamma)X^{\alpha}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+\beta(\gamma-\alpha)X^{\beta}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+\gamma(\alpha-\beta)X^{\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}\Big)

\displaystyle=(\beta-\alpha)(\gamma-\delta)X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{\gamma+\delta}+(\gamma-\alpha)(\delta-\beta)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\delta}+(\delta-\alpha)(\beta-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\delta}Y^{\beta+\gamma}

\displaystyle+(\beta-\gamma)(\delta-\alpha)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\delta}+(\beta-\delta)(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\beta+\delta}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+(\delta-\gamma)(\alpha-\beta)X^{\gamma+\delta}Y^{\alpha+\beta}.

Now, using

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \alpha=1+\lambda_{1}\\
\beta=1-\lambda_{1}\\
\gamma=1+\lambda_{2}\\
\delta=1-\lambda_{2}\end{aligned}\right.

the expression becomes

\displaystyle\det(B)=(-2\lambda_{1})(2\lambda_{2})X^{2}Y^{2}+(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(-\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1})X^{2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle+(-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})X^{2+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+(-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})(-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})X^{2-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle+(-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})X^{2-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+(-2\lambda_{2})(2\lambda_{1})X^{2}Y^{2}

\displaystyle=-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(X^{2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}+X^{2-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(X^{2+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+X^{2-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\right)-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}X^{2}Y^{2}

\displaystyle=X^{2}Y^{2}\left((\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(\left(\frac{X}{Y}\right)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}+\left(\frac{Y}{X}\right)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\right)-\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}\left(\left(\frac{X}{Y}\right)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+\left(\frac{Y}{X}\right)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\right)-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\det(A)=a^{2}b^{2}\left((\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\right)-\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}\left(\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}\right)-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\right).

(2.38)

This leads us to study (for all
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>0
) the function

\displaystyle g(x)=\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}\left(x^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}+\frac{1}{x^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}}\right)-\left(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}\left(x^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}+\frac{1}{x^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}}\right)-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}.

Notice that

\displaystyle g(1)=2(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})^{2}-2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=0.

We will show that
g(x)<0
for all
x>1
(or equivalently
0<x<1
by symmetry). In fact, we find it make convenient to work with

\displaystyle f(x)=x^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}g(x)=\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}\left(x^{2\lambda_{1}}+x^{2\lambda_{2}}\right)-(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(x^{2\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}}+1\right)-8\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}x^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}

For notational convenience, write
\alpha=\lambda_{1}
and
\beta=\lambda_{2}
, so that

\displaystyle f(x)=(\alpha+\beta)^{2}(x^{2\alpha}+x^{2\beta})-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}(x^{2\alpha+2\beta}+1)-8\alpha\beta\,x^{\alpha+\beta}.

We have

\displaystyle xf^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\beta)^{2}(2\alpha\,x^{2\alpha}+2\beta\,x^{2\beta})-2(\alpha+\beta)(\alpha-\beta)^{2}x^{2\alpha+2\beta}-8\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)x^{\alpha+\beta}

\displaystyle x\frac{d}{dx}\left(xf^{\prime}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=(\alpha+\beta)^{2}(4\alpha^{2}x^{2\alpha}+4\beta^{2}x^{2\beta})-4(\alpha+\beta)^{2}(\alpha-\beta)^{2}x^{2\alpha+2\beta}-8\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)^{2}x^{\alpha+\beta}

\displaystyle=4(\alpha+\beta)^{2}\left(\alpha^{2}x^{2\alpha}+\beta^{2}x^{2\beta}-2\alpha\beta\,x^{\alpha+\beta}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}x^{2\alpha+2\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=4(\alpha+\beta)^{2}\left((\alpha\,x^{\alpha}-\beta\,x^{\beta})^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}x^{2\beta+2\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=4(\alpha+\beta)^{2}\left(\alpha\,x^{\alpha}-\beta\,x^{\beta}+(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha+\beta}\right)\left(\alpha\,x^{\alpha}-\beta\,x^{\beta}-(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha+\beta}\right)

\displaystyle=-4(\alpha+\beta)^{2}x^{\beta}(\alpha\,x^{\alpha}-\beta\,x^{\beta}+(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha+\beta})((\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha}-\alpha\,x^{\alpha-\beta}+\beta).

We are led to study the function

\displaystyle h(x)=(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha}-\alpha\,x^{\alpha-\beta}+\beta.

We have

\displaystyle x\,h^{\prime}(x)=\alpha(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha}-\alpha(\alpha-\beta)x^{\alpha-\beta}>0

(2.39)

for all
x>1
. Therefore,
h
is a strictly increasing function on
(1,\infty)
, and since
h(1)=0
, we deduce that
h(x)>0
for all
x>1
, which implies that

\displaystyle x\dfrac{d}{dx}(xf^{\prime}(x))<0\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x>1.

Therefore,
x\mapstochar\rightarrow xf^{\prime}(x)
is strictly decreasing on
(1,\infty)
, and since

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(1)=2(\alpha+\beta)^{3}-2(\alpha+\beta)(\alpha-\beta)^{2}-8\alpha\beta(\alpha+\beta)=2(\alpha+\beta)\left((\alpha+\beta)^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}-4\alpha\beta\right)=0,

we deduce that
f^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x>1
, which finally implies that
f
is a strictly decreasing function on
(1,\infty)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle f(x)<0\qquad\text{for all}\;\,x>1.

Coming back to (
2.38
), we deduce that
\det(A)<0
, which implies that
Y=0
.

Case 2:

\lambda=(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}
.

Then, the roots are given by
r_{3}=r_{4}=1
, and
r_{1}=1+\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}=1+\lambda_{1}
,
r_{2}=1-\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}=1-\lambda_{1}
. Therefore, the solution
Y
is given by

\displaystyle Y(t)=\mu_{1}e^{(1+\lambda_{1})t}+\mu_{2}e^{(1-\lambda_{1})t}+\mu_{3}e^{t}+\mu_{4}\,t\,e^{t}.

The boundary conditions (
2.34
) are equivalent to

\displaystyle A\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\\
\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{4}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{1+\lambda_{1}}&a^{1-\lambda_{1}}&a&a\log(a)\\
b^{1+\lambda_{1}}&b^{1-\lambda_{1}}&b&b\log(b)\\
(1+\lambda_{1})a^{1+\lambda_{1}}&(1-\lambda_{1})a^{1-\lambda_{1}}&a&(a+1)\log(a)\\
(1+\lambda_{1})b^{1+\lambda_{1}}&(1-\lambda_{1})b^{1-\lambda_{1}}&b&(b+1)\log(b)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\\
\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{4}\end{pmatrix}=0.

(2.40)

Let
X,Y\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{C}
, and define the following matrix

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}&\log(X)X^{\gamma}\\
Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}&\log(Y)Y^{\gamma}\\
\alpha X^{\alpha}&\beta X^{\beta}&\gamma X^{\gamma}&(1+\gamma\log(X))X^{\gamma}\\
\alpha Y^{\alpha}&\beta Y^{\beta}&\gamma Y^{\gamma}&(1+\gamma\log(Y))Y^{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}.

Expanding on the fourth column, we get

\displaystyle\det(B)=-\log(X)X^{\gamma}\begin{vmatrix}Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}\\
\alpha X^{\alpha}&\beta X^{\beta}&\gamma X^{\gamma}\\
\alpha Y^{\alpha}&\beta Y^{\beta}&\gamma Y^{\gamma}\end{vmatrix}+\log(Y)Y^{\gamma}\begin{vmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}\\
\alpha X^{\alpha}&\beta X^{\beta}&\gamma X^{\gamma}\\
\alpha Y^{\alpha}&\beta Y^{\beta}&\gamma Y^{\gamma}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle-\left(1+\gamma\log(X)\right)X^{\gamma}\begin{vmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}\\
Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}\\
\alpha Y^{\alpha}&\beta Y^{\beta}&\gamma Y^{\gamma}\end{vmatrix}+\left(1+\gamma\log(Y)\right)Y^{\gamma}\begin{vmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}\\
Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}\\
\alpha X^{\alpha}&\beta X^{\beta}&\gamma X^{\gamma}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\log(X)\left(\beta(\gamma-\alpha)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+\cancel{\gamma(\alpha-\beta)X^{2\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}}+\alpha(\beta-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle-\log(X)\left(\gamma(\gamma-\beta)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+\gamma(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+\cancel{\gamma(\beta-\alpha)X^{2\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle+\log(Y)\left(\cancel{\gamma(\beta-\alpha)X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{2\gamma}}+\alpha(\gamma-\beta)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+\beta(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle+\log(Y)\left(\gamma(\gamma-\alpha)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+\cancel{\gamma(\alpha-\beta)X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{2\gamma}}+\gamma(\beta-\gamma)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle-\left((\gamma-\beta)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+(\beta-\alpha)X^{2\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+(\gamma-\alpha)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+(\alpha-\beta)X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{2\gamma}+(\beta-\gamma)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\log(X)\Big((\beta-\gamma)(\gamma-\alpha)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle+\log(Y)\Big((\alpha-\gamma)(\gamma-\beta)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+(\beta-\gamma)(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle+(\beta-\alpha)\Big(X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}-\left(X^{2\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}+X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{2\gamma}\right)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\alpha-\gamma)(\beta-\gamma)\left(X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}-X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}\right)\log\left(\frac{Y}{X}\right)

\displaystyle+(\alpha-\beta)\Big(X^{2\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\beta}+X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{2\gamma}-\big(X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}\big)\Big).

(2.41)

Taking now

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\alpha=1+\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}=1+\lambda_{1}\\
&\beta=1-\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}=1-\lambda_{1}\\
&\gamma=1\end{aligned}\right.

(2.42)

we get (if
X=a
and
Y=b
)

\displaystyle\det(A)

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{1}^{2}\left(a^{2+\lambda_{1}}b^{2-\lambda_{1}}-a^{2-\lambda_{1}}b^{2+\lambda_{1}}\right)\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)+2\lambda_{1}\left(a^{2}b^{2}+a^{2}b^{2}-\left(a^{2+\lambda_{1}}b^{2-\lambda_{1}}+a^{2-\lambda_{1}}b^{2+\lambda_{1}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}(ab)^{2}\left(4-2\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}+\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\lambda_{1}}\right)+\lambda_{1}\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}-\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\lambda_{1}}\right)\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right).

(2.43)

We are therefore led to consider on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
the function

\displaystyle g(x)=2\left((1+x)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\beta}}\right)-\beta\left((1+x)^{\beta}-\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\beta}}\right)\log(1+x)-4

where
\beta=\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}\geq\sqrt{2}
. Notice that
f(0)=0
. Then, we compute

\displaystyle(1+x)g^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\beta\left((1+x)^{\beta}-\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\beta}}\right)-\beta^{2}\left((1+x)^{\beta}+\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\beta}}\right)\log(1+x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2(1+x)^{\beta}}\left(2((1+x)^{2\beta}-1)-2\beta\left((1+x)^{2\beta}+1\right)\log(1+x)\right).

Letting
\alpha=2\beta
, we are now led to study the function
Let
\alpha\geq\sqrt{2}
and consider

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=2\left((1+x)^{\alpha}-1\right)-\alpha\left((1+x)^{\alpha}+1\right)\log(1+x).

We have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=2\alpha(1+x)^{\alpha-1}-\alpha^{2}(1+x)^{\alpha-1}\log(1+x)-\alpha(1+x)^{\alpha-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha(1+x)^{\alpha-1}(1-\alpha\log(1+x))-\frac{\alpha}{1+x}=-\alpha(1+x)^{\alpha-1}\left(\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\alpha}}+\alpha\log(1+x)-1\right).

Now, let

\displaystyle h(x)=\frac{1}{(1+x)^{\alpha}}+\alpha\log(1+x)-1.

We have

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{\alpha}{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{\alpha}{1+x}=\frac{\alpha}{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}}\left((1+x)^{\alpha}-1\right)\geq 0.

Therefore,
h
is an increasing function, which shows that

\displaystyle h(x)>h(0)=0

for all
x>0
. In particular,
f^{\prime}(x)\leq 0
, which shows that
f
is decreasing and
f(x)\leq f(0)=0
(and even
f(x)<0
for all
x>0
). Therefore, we deduce that
g^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x>0
, which finally implies that
\det(A)<0
. As a consequence, our solution
Y
vanishes identically.

Case 3:

\lambda>(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2}
.

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} r_{1}&=1+\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}+m^{2}+n^{2}}\\
r_{2}&=1-\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}+m^{2}+n^{2}}\\
r_{3}&=1+i\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})}\\
r_{4}&=1-i\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})},\end{aligned}\right.

and if
\lambda_{1}=\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}+m^{2}+n^{2}}
and
\lambda_{2}=\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})}
, there exists
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu_{3},\mu_{4}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle Y(t)=\mu_{1}e^{(1+\lambda_{1})t}+\mu_{2}e^{(1-\lambda_{1})t}+\mu_{3}e^{(1+i\,\lambda_{2})t}+\mu_{4}e^{(1-i\,\lambda_{2})t}

(2.44)

we get thanks to (
2.34
) the system

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a^{1+\lambda_{1}}&a^{1-\lambda_{1}}&a^{1+i\,\lambda_{2}}&a^{1-i\,\lambda_{2}}\\
b^{1+\lambda_{1}}&b^{1-\lambda_{1}}&b^{1+i\,\lambda_{2}}&b^{1-i\,\lambda_{2}}\\
(1+\lambda_{1})a^{1+\lambda_{1}}&(1-\lambda_{1})a^{1-\lambda_{1}}&(1+i\,\lambda_{2})a^{1+i\,\lambda_{2}}&(1-i\,\lambda_{2})a^{1-i\,\lambda_{2}}\\
(1+\lambda_{1})b^{1+\lambda_{1}}&(1-\lambda_{1})b^{1-\lambda_{1}}&(1+i\,\lambda_{2})b^{1+i\,\lambda_{2}}&(1-i\,\lambda_{2})b^{1-i\,\lambda_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\\
\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{4}\end{pmatrix}=0.

(2.45)

The underlying matrix
A
falls is a specific case of the matrix studied in
Case 1
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\begin{vmatrix}X^{\alpha}&X^{\beta}&X^{\gamma}&X^{\delta}\\
Y^{\alpha}&Y^{\beta}&Y^{\gamma}&Y^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,X^{\alpha}&\beta\,X^{\beta}&\gamma\,X^{\gamma}&\delta\,X^{\delta}\\
\alpha\,Y^{\alpha}&\beta\,Y^{\beta}&\gamma\,Y^{\gamma}&\delta\,Y^{\delta}\end{vmatrix}

\displaystyle=(\beta-\alpha)(\gamma-\delta)X^{\alpha+\beta}Y^{\gamma+\delta}+(\gamma-\alpha)(\delta-\beta)X^{\alpha+\gamma}Y^{\beta+\delta}

\displaystyle+(\delta-\alpha)(\beta-\gamma)X^{\alpha+\delta}Y^{\beta+\gamma}+(\beta-\gamma)(\delta-\alpha)X^{\beta+\gamma}Y^{\alpha+\delta}

\displaystyle+(\beta-\delta)(\alpha-\gamma)X^{\beta+\delta}Y^{\alpha+\gamma}+(\delta-\gamma)(\alpha-\beta)X^{\gamma+\delta}Y^{\alpha+\beta}.

Letting

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \alpha&=1+\lambda_{1}\\
\beta&=1-\lambda_{1}\\
\gamma&=1+i\,\lambda_{2}\\
\delta&=1-i\,\lambda_{2}\end{aligned}\right.

and
(X,Y)=(a,b)
, we get

\displaystyle\det(A)=-2\lambda_{1}(2\,i\,\lambda_{2})X^{2}Y^{2}+(i\,\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(-i\,\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1})X^{2+\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle+(-i\,\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})(-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2})X^{2+\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}+(-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2})(-i\,\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})X^{2-\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle+(-\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2})(\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2})X^{2-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}+(-2i\,\lambda_{2})(2\lambda_{1})X^{2}Y^{2}

\displaystyle=-8\,i\,\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}X^{2}Y^{2}-(\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(X^{2+\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}+X^{2-\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2})^{2}\left(X^{2+\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2-\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}+X^{2-\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}}Y^{2+\lambda_{1}-i\,\lambda_{2}}\right),

and finally, if
\zeta=\lambda_{1}+i\,\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle\det(A)=a^{2}b^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}\left(\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\overline{\zeta}}+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\overline{\zeta}}\right)-\overline{\zeta}^{2}\left(\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\zeta}+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\zeta}\right)-8\,i\,\mathrm{Re}\,(\zeta)\mathrm{Im}\,(\zeta)\right).

(2.46)

We could have algebraically replaced
\lambda_{2}
by
i\,\lambda_{2}
in (
2.38
). Now, it is apparent that

\displaystyle\det(A)=2\,i\,a^{2}b^{2}\left(\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\zeta^{2}\left(\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\overline{\zeta}}+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\overline{\zeta}}\right)\right)-4\,\mathrm{Re}\,(\zeta)\mathrm{Im}\,(\zeta)\right).

Now, we have for all
t>0

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\,\left(\zeta^{2}t^{\overline{\zeta}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\,\left((\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}+2\,i\,\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})t^{\lambda_{1}}\left(\cos(\lambda_{2}\log(t))-i\,\sin(\lambda_{2}\log(t))\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-(\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2})t^{\lambda_{1}}\sin(\lambda_{2}\log(t))+2\,\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}t^{\lambda_{1}}\cos\left(\lambda_{2}\log(t)\right).

Therefore, we deduce that
\det(A)=0
if and only if

\displaystyle-(\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2})\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\sin\left(\lambda_{2}\log\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)\right)+2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\lambda_{1}}\cos\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-(\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2})\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\sin\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)+2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\cos\left(\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)-4\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=0,

that we finally rewrite as

\displaystyle 2\left(\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}+\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\lambda_{1}}\right)\cos\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)-2\right)\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}-\left(\lambda_{1}^{2}-\lambda_{2}^{2}\right)\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}+\bigg(\frac{a}{b}\bigg)^{\lambda_{1}}\right)\sin\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)=0.

(2.47)

This is the universal equation that will keep appearing in all dimension and for all operators
\mathscr{L}_{m}
(where
m\geq 1
).
Finally, we deduce that this equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left(\left(1+\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\lambda_{1}}\right)\cos\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right)-2\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\right)\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}=(m^{2}+n^{2})\left(\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2\lambda_{1}}-1\right)\sin\left(\lambda_{2}\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\right).

(2.48)

First, make the change of variable
R=\log\left(\dfrac{b}{a}\right)
. We have

\lambda_{1}=\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}}
,
which leads us to introduce the following function

\displaystyle\varphi(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(1+e^{2R\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}+t^{2}}}\right)\cos(Rt)-2\,e^{R\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}+t^{2}}}\right)t\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}+t^{2}}

\displaystyle-m^{2}\left(e^{2R\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}+t^{2}}}-1\right)\sin(Rt).

(2.49)

Make a second change of variable
Rt=\theta
, and define
\varphi(t)=\psi(\theta)
so that

\displaystyle\psi(\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(1+e^{2\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\cos(\theta)-2\,e^{\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\frac{\theta}{R}\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})+\left(\frac{\theta^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle-m^{2}\left(e^{2\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-1\right)\sin(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R^{2}}\left(\left(1+e^{2\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\cos(\theta)-2\,e^{\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\theta\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}

\displaystyle-(m^{2}+n^{2})\left(e^{2\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-1\right)\sin(\theta).

(2.50)

In order to simplify notations, let

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} a^{2}&=(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}\\
b&=\frac{1}{R}\\
c^{2}&=m^{2}+n^{2}\end{aligned}\right.

(2.51)

so that

\displaystyle\psi(\theta)=b^{2}\left(\left(1+e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\cos(\theta)-2\,e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\theta\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}-c^{2}\left(e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-1\right)\sin(\theta).

(2.52)

As previously, we want to estimate the first strictly positive zero of
\psi
. Notice that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\psi(2\pi)=b^{2}\left(e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+(2\pi)^{2}}}-1\right)^{2}2\pi\sqrt{a^{2}+(2\pi)^{2}}>0\\
&\psi(\pi)=-b^{2}\left(e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\pi^{2}}}+1\right)^{2}\pi\sqrt{a^{2}+\pi^{2}}<0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.53)

Therefore, we will show that
\psi(\theta)<0
for all
0<\theta\leq\pi
, which will imply that the first non-trivial zero
\alpha
of
\psi
satisfies the inequality
\pi<\alpha<2\pi
. First, notice that since
\cos(\theta)\leq 0
for all
\dfrac{\pi}{2}\leq\theta\leq\pi
, we have

\displaystyle\psi(\theta)\leq-2\,e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\theta\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}<0\quad\text{for all}\;\,\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\theta\leq\pi.

(2.54)

Therefore, the interval
\left[\dfrac{\pi}{2},\pi\right]
is a zero-free region of
\psi
, and we need only show that
\psi(\theta)<0
on
\left(0,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right)
.

We have

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=b^{2}\left(\frac{2\theta}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\cos(\theta)-\left(1+e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\sin(\theta)-\frac{2\theta}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\theta\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}

\displaystyle+b^{2}\left(\left(1+e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\cos(\theta)-2e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)\left(\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{\theta^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2c^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\sin(\theta)-c^{2}\left(e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-1\right)\cos(\theta)

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta)\left(e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\left(2b^{2}\theta^{2}+b^{2}\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-c^{2}\right)+b^{2}\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}\theta^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}+c^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\theta\sin(\theta)\left(e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\left(\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{2c^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)+\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle-2b^{2}e^{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\left(\theta^{2}+\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{\theta^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta)h_{1}(\theta)-\theta\sin(\theta)h_{2}(\theta)-h_{3}(\theta).

(2.55)

We trivially have for all
\theta\in\left[0,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]
the inequality

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\theta)\leq\cos(\theta)h_{1}(\theta),

where

\displaystyle h_{1}(\theta)=e^{2\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}\left(2b^{2}\theta^{2}+b^{2}\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-c^{2}\right)+b^{2}\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}\theta^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}+c^{2}.

(2.56)

Now, consider the function

\displaystyle f(\theta)

\displaystyle=2b^{2}\theta^{2}+b^{2}\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-c^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\theta^{2}}{R^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}}\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}-(m^{2}+n^{2}).

Using the elementary inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
(valid for all
x,y\geq 0
), we deduce that for all
\theta\in\left[0,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]

\displaystyle f(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{2R^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}}\left(\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}\,R+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{R^{3}\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})

(2.57)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2R^{3}\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}\left(-(2m^{2}+2n^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}R^{3}+2(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\pi(\pi+1)\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R+2\right).

(2.58)

Now, let
P_{m,n}
be the following polynomial function

\displaystyle P_{m,n}(R)=-(2m^{2}+2n^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}R^{3}+2(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\pi(\pi+1)\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R+2.

Making the change of variable
s=\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R
, if
Q(\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R)=P(R)
, we get

\displaystyle Q(s)=-s^{3}+2s^{2}+\pi(\pi+1)s+2.

Let us estimate the roots of
Q
.

Figure 1:
Numerical Approximation of the Roots of
Q
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} Q(-3)&=27+18-3\pi(\pi+1)+2=47-3\pi(\pi+1)>0\\
Q(-2)&=8+8-2\pi(\pi+1)+2=18-2\pi(\pi+1)<0\\
Q(-1)&=5-\pi(\pi+1)<0\\
Q(0)&=2>0\\
Q(4)&=-64+32+4\pi(\pi+1)+2=2(-15+2\pi(\pi+1))>0\\
Q(5)&=-125+50+5\pi(\pi+1)+2=-73+5\pi(\pi+1)<0,\end{aligned}\right.

where we only used the elementary inequality
13<\pi(\pi+1)<14
. Therefore,
Q

admits three real roots
r_{1}<r_{2}<r_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -3&<r_{1}<-2\\
-1&<r_{2}<0\\
4&<r_{3}<5.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.59)

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(s)=-3s^{2}+4s+\pi(\pi+1),

and the roots of
Q^{\prime}
are given by

\displaystyle r_{\pm}=\frac{2}{3}\pm\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{4+3\pi(\pi+1)}<4,

using again the elementary inequality
\pi(\pi+1)<14
and the inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
. In particular, we deduce that
Q
is strictly decreasing on
(4,\infty)
, which implies that

\displaystyle Q(s)\leq Q(5)=-73+5\pi(\pi+1)<0\quad\text{for all}\;\,s\geq 5.

Therefore, coming back to
P_{m,n}(R)=Q(\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R)
, we deduce that for all
\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R\geq 5
, we have

\displaystyle P_{m,n}(R)\leq P_{m,n}\left(\frac{5}{\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}\right)=-(73-5\pi(\pi+1))\quad\text{for all}\;\,R\geq\frac{5}{\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}.

(2.60)

Therefore, provided that the following inequality holds

\displaystyle R\geq\frac{5}{\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}},

(2.61)

we deduce that for all
\theta\in\left[0,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]
,

\displaystyle f(\theta)\leq-\frac{73-5\pi(\pi+1)}{2R^{3}\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}.

(2.62)

Therefore, by (
2.56
) and (
2.62
), we deduce that for all
R
satisfying the inequality (
2.61
), we have

\displaystyle h_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{73-5\pi(\pi+1)}{2R^{3}\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}e^{2\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}}\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}+\frac{\theta^{2}}{R^{2}\sqrt{(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+\theta^{2}}}

\displaystyle+(m^{2}+n^{2})

which converges as
R\rightarrow\infty
to
-\infty
uniformly in
\theta\in\left[0,\dfrac{\pi}{2}\right]
. More precisely, we estimate

\displaystyle h_{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{73-5\pi(\pi+1)}{2R^{3}\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}e^{2\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}\,R}+\frac{\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}}{R}+\frac{\pi}{2R^{2}}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R^{3}}+(m^{2}+n^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R^{3}}\left(4(m^{2}+n^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}R^{3}+4(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+2\pi\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2(73-5\pi(\pi+1))e^{2\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R}+\pi^{2}\right).

We are therefore let to study the function

\displaystyle f(R)=4(m^{2}+n^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}R^{3}+4(2m^{2}+2n^{2})R^{2}+2\pi\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R+\pi^{2}-2(73-5\pi(\pi+1))e^{2\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R}

Once more, making a change of variable
s=\sqrt{2m^{2}+2n^{2}}R
, we are reduced to studying the function

\displaystyle g(s)=4s^{3}+4s^{2}+2\pi s+\pi^{2}-2(73-5\pi(\pi+1))e^{2s}.

Let us show that
g(s)<0
for all
s\geq 0
. Indeed, we have for all
s\geq 0

\displaystyle e^{2s}\geq 1+2s+\frac{(2s)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2s)^{3}}{6}=1+2s+2s^{2}+\frac{4}{3}s^{3},

and we easily check that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} 2(73-5\pi(\pi+1))&>\pi^{2}\\
4(73-5\pi(\pi+1))&>2\pi\\
4(73-5\pi(\pi+1))&>4\\
\frac{8}{3}(73-5\pi(\pi+1))&>4.\end{aligned}\right.

Therefore, we finally deduce that for all
R
such that (
2.61
) holds true we have

\displaystyle h_{1}(\theta)\leq 0\quad\text{for all}\;\,\theta\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right],

which implies by (
2.7
) that
\psi
is strictly decreasing on
[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
, which shows by (
2.53
) that
\psi(\theta)<0
for all
0<\theta\leq\pi
. Therefore, we finally deduce by (
2.53
) that the first non-trivial zero
\theta_{1}>0
of
\psi
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\pi<\theta_{1}<2\pi,

Coming back to the equation (
2.48
), we deduce since
R=\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)
,
R\lambda_{2}=\theta

that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})}=\lambda_{2}=\frac{\theta_{1}}{R}>\frac{\pi}{\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}.

Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\sqrt{\lambda+4m^{2}n^{2}}-(m^{2}+n^{2})>\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)},

or

\displaystyle\lambda>\left((m^{2}+n^{2})+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)^{2}-4m^{2}n^{2}=\left((m-n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left((m+n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right).

(2.63)

Step 4: Lower estimate of all eigenvalue when
m
is a strictly positive integer.
Assume from now on that
m\geq 1
is a positive integer.

Since

\displaystyle\left((m-n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\left((m+n)^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{\log^{2}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)}\right)\underset{\frac{b}{a}\rightarrow\infty}{\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow}(m^{2}-n^{2})^{2},

we deducet that the quantity on the right-hand side of (
2.63
) is minimal when
n=m
or
n=-m
if the conformal class is large enough. First, notice that (
2.61
) holds for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
if and only if

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{5}{\sqrt{2m^{2}}}=\frac{5}{\sqrt{2}\,m}.

(2.64)

Therefore, assume that (
2.64
) holds.
Indeed, let
\alpha>0
, and define

\displaystyle f(t)=\left((m-t)^{2}+\alpha^{2}\right)\left((m+t)^{2}+\alpha^{2}\right)=(m^{2}-t^{2})^{2}+2(m^{2}+t^{2})\alpha^{2}+\alpha^{4}.

This is a strictly convex function, which therefore admits a unique minimum. We have

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)=-4\,t\,(m^{2}-t^{2})+4\,t\,\alpha^{2}=4t(t^{2}+\alpha^{2}-m^{2}).

If
\alpha^{2}-m^{2}\geq 0
, or
\alpha\geq m
, then
f
admits its minimum at
t=0
, while for
\alpha<m
,
f
is minimal at
t=\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}
and
t=-\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle f(0)=(m^{2}+\alpha^{2})^{2}=m^{4}+2\,\alpha^{2}m^{2}+\alpha^{4}

\displaystyle f(m)=\alpha^{2}(4m^{2}+\alpha^{2})=4\,\alpha^{2}m^{2}+\alpha^{4},

which shows that

\displaystyle f(0)-f(m)=m^{4}-2\alpha^{2}m^{2}=m^{2}(m^{2}-2\alpha^{2})>0

(2.65)

provided that
m>\alpha\sqrt{2}
. We therefore assume from now on that

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)\geq\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{m}.

(2.66)

Furthermore, notice that
f
is decreasing on
[0,\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}})
and increasing on
[\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}},\infty[
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}f(n)=\min\left\{f\left([\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}]\right),f\left([\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}]+1\right)\right\}.

(2.67)

We have
[\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}]=m-1
if and only if
\alpha^{2}\leq 2m-1
. Then, we get

\displaystyle\min\left\{f\left([\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}]\right),f\left([\sqrt{m^{2}-\alpha^{2}}]+1\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{f(m-1),f(m)\right\}=\min\left\{(1+\alpha^{2})((2m-1)^{2}+\alpha^{2}),(4m^{2}+\alpha^{2})\alpha^{2}\right\}.

We have

\displaystyle g(\alpha)=(1+\alpha^{2})((2m-1)^{2}+\alpha^{2})-(4m^{2}+\alpha^{2})\alpha^{2}=(2m-1)^{2}-2(2m-1)\alpha^{2}\geq 0

if and only if
